To fix Power Analysis Check

What we found

We detected 1 potential power analysis.

Show details

We used the LLM model ‘vllm/qwen3-8-27b-fp8’ to check the contents of 2 paragraphs that contained words suggesting they might contain power analyses.

Some essential information could not be detected: alpha_level, power, effect_size, software

power_type statistical_test sample_size alpha_level power effect_size effect_size_metric software
posthoc other 34 NA NA NA other NA

You reported a power analysis that has been classified as ‘post-hoc’. Calculating observed power is almost never useful. If you actually performed a sensitivity power analysis, label it as such explicitly.

text
These mode-matched, not token-budget-matched, contrasts are in Supplementary Note S38. Physician safety ratings are inconclusive Three physicians rated seed-1 lists for all 34 top-1-discordant original-cohort cases. S1 danger (mean AC1 0•185), dangerous-omission (0•423/0•458), and D5 preference (0•196) missed the 0•6 agreement gate; only misleading-inclusion ratings met it (0•834/0•726). The safety comparison is therefore inconclusive. Descriptively, 23 of 34 majority verdicts were ties (non-tied 6:5; MDTmore-dangerous rate 0•545, 95% CI 0•234 to 0•833); omission flags favoured A×1 (26•5% versus 14•7%), misleading-inclusion flags MDT (0% versus 14•7%), but power covered only preferences beyond ~72% (Supplementary Figure S2d; Supplementary Note S37). On the replacement cohort the redesigned rating passed the gate for every family except D5 preference (mean AC1 0•836 danger, 0•819/0•992 omission/inclusion flags, 0•822 rescue; D5 0•467, descriptive), with perfect intra-rater agreement (14/14 embedded repeats each). The primary question-did the losing list contain an acceptable entry encompassing the reference? was answered yes for 29 of 35 team-lost cases (82•9%) and 18 of 18 single-call-lost cases (100%); the -17•1-point difference (95% Newcombe CI -32•7 to +2•6) leaves the forceddifferentiation mechanism neither validated nor refuted, in the direction opposite to the hypothesis (baseline rescue on concordant misses 6/15, 40%). The secondary danger comparison was null (MDT more dangerous in 3 of 11 non-tied units, p = 0•23; omission flags 8:3, p = 0•23; misleading inclusion 0:0; no side preference in controls) (Supplementary Note S39).

Power analyses need to contain the following information to be interpretable: the type of power analysis, the statistical test, the software used, sample size, critical alpha criterion, power level, effect size, and an effect size metric. In addition, it is recommended to make sure the power analysis is reproducible (by sharing the code, or a screenshot, of the power analysis), and to provide good arguments for why the study was designed to detect an effect of this size.

For an a-priori power analysis, where the sample size is determined, reporting all information would look like:

An a priori power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that for a Cohen’s d = 0.5, an alpha level of 0.05, and a desired power level of 80% required at least 64 participants in each group.

For a sensitivity power analysis, this sentence would look like:

A sensitivity power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that with 64 participants in each group, and an alpha level of 0.05, a desired power level of 80% was reached for an effect size of d = 0.5.

Working Directory (setwd)

Best programming practice is to avoid setwd() in analysis code: it hardcodes an assumption about the working directory (often an absolute path on the author’s own machine), so the code breaks when run anywhere else. Keep the working directory as the caller sets it and use relative paths. No setwd() calls were found in any of the code files.

Package Installation (install.packages)

Best programming practice is to avoid install.packages() in analysis code: it installs software on whoever runs the script without asking, which is at minimum disruptive (an unexpected install, network access, a package version the author never tested against) and at worst a security risk if it points at an untrusted repository. Document required packages instead (a README, or a list of them: an renv.lock/DESCRIPTION for R, a requirements.txt for Python). No install.packages() calls were found in any of the code files.

Libraries / Imports

Best programming practice is to load all required libraries/imports in one block near the top of the code. In 142 code files, libraries/imports were at multiple places (i.e., with more than 3 non-comment lines in between).

How to make your code run on another computer

We ran your code on a clean computer, the way a reader or reviewer would. Below is each problem we found, the scripts it affects, and how to fix it. Fix them in the order given: a problem near the top can cause the ones below it.

Files are missing from your deposit.

  • ax1_t03_mcr.py reads medcasereasoning_subset.json.
  • ax1_t03_sensitivity.py reads .env.
  • erreason_ax1t03_seeds.py reads topn_erreason_ax1t03.jsonl.
  • ax1_cot_cpc.py reads .env.
  • ds_mcr_ax1_gonogo.py reads medcasereasoning_subset.json.
  • er_contract_s1_preview.py reads er_reason_subset.json.
  • er_gonogo_f1_qwen.py reads .env.
  • gen_ax1_zdr_missing.py reads Ax1_ds_s1.jsonl.
  • run_scheme_b_group1.py reads mgh_qa_dataset_group_1.json.
  • s1_preview.py reads Ax1_s1.jsonl.
  • and 122 more

The script stops at the line that reads the file.

  1. Find the file on your computer.
  2. Add it to the deposit, in the folder where the script looks for it.
  3. If you cannot share it, for example for privacy reasons, say so in the README, and explain where readers can get it and where to put it.

A script uses a location that exists only on your computer.

  • make_figure5.py reads /routing_study/results/data/judge_caches/judge_cache_glm_v3.json.
  • make_main_figures.py reads /routing_study/results/token_costs/token_costs.json.

No other computer has that folder, and the file is also not in the deposit.

  1. Add the file to the deposit.
  2. In the script, write the location from the top folder of your project, such as data/survey.csv. In R, here::here(“data”, “survey.csv”) does this for you.
  3. Remove any setwd() lines.

A script could not open a file, but the error does not say which.

  • word_coverage_cpc_mcr.py

Check every file the script reads. Make sure each one is in the deposit, and that its location is written from the top folder of the project.

A package was not installed.

  • app.py

The code loads it in a way we could not detect before running it. List every package with its version, in renv.lock (R) or requirements.txt (Python), and load each one at the top of the script with library() or import.

A script uses something it does not create or load itself.

  • v26_condense_chain.py: os

Usually the object was still in your R session from earlier work, or another script made it.

  1. Restart R (in RStudio: Session > Restart R).
  2. Run each script from top to bottom, and fix what is missing.
  3. If a script needs results of another, save them to a file in the first script and read them in the second.
  4. Load every package with library() at the top.

R or Python could not read a script, because of a mistake in the code itself.

  • overnight_stability.py

Run each script once in a fresh session before you deposit it; the error message shows the line to fix.

A script runs longer than our limit of 5 minutes.

  • mcr_prejudge.py

State in the README how long each script runs. If some steps take hours, save their results (such as fitted models) to files, add those to the deposit, and let the script read them, so readers can check the rest without waiting.

A script downloads data while it runs.

  • e_legal_prepare.py
  • index_novb.py

Online files can move, change or disappear. Save the downloaded files, add them to the deposit, and let the script read the saved copies.

A script waits for a person to choose a file, answer a question, or give arguments on the command line.

  • v26_uniform_chain.py
  • v27_repair_infer.py
  • batch_ablation.py
  • er_lenient_rescore.py
  • er_seeds_clean.py
  • harvest.py
  • judge_shards.py
  • judge_validity_app.py
  • local_diagnose.py
  • rejudge_results.py

Nobody can answer when the code is checked automatically. Write the file name into the script, and put the exact command with its arguments in the README.

Metacheck can’t automatically establish what caused the error, or how it should be improved.

  • er_ds_ax1_zdr.py
  • s1_call_ledger.py
  • top3_groupmain.py
  • e_gpt51_er.py
  • pmc392_ds_sensitivity.py
  • compare_results.py
  • curate_pilot.py

Comparison with the paper

Of the 15 statistical tests reported in the paper, 0 were found in the output of the code.

All details of the Reproducibility Check

Python code

This module also assesses whether the paper’s Python code could be run on its data, and — because execute = TRUE — actually ran it. We examined 201 Python code files.

Run order

The scripts were ordered by their data dependencies (a script writing a file another reads runs first) and numeric filename prefixes. Python has no source()-equivalent import-based ordering signal (an import loads a module, not the paper’s own analysis flow), so that basis never applies here.

Undefined-name errors

1 script failed on an undefined name (NameError). This typically means the script expects a variable, function, or class that another script defines — i.e. it is meant to be run alongside a larger set of scripts, not on its own — or the name is simply never created. When another of the paper’s own script files defines the same name, this is called out below as a likely ordering/copy-paste gap. Each is counted as an error (red).

File Missing name Defined in
v26_condense_chain.py os (not defined anywhere in the paper’s code)

1 script uses Windows paths, with \ between folders, which works only on Windows. To run the code we put / instead, which works on every system, Windows included.

| gpt5.1 | CPC | None | None | None |

An ethics approval statement was detected, based on the following text:

This study is a secondary analysis of existing, de-identified data with no human-subjects recruitment and no access to identifiable information, and the institutional ethics committee reviewed the protocol and granted an exemption from institutional review board review (No.

It was conducted in accordance with the ethical standards of the 1964 Declaration of Helsinki and its later amendments.

How Ethics Approval Check works

This module searches for statements of ethics approval, IRB approval, institutional review board approval, and related terms.

Patterns are designed to capture:

Human research committee names - Institutional Review Board / IRB (US standard) - Independent Ethics Committee / IEC (ICH-GCP international clinical trial term) - Ethical/Ethics Review Board (generic) - Ethics (Advisory/Review) Committee/Board/Panel/Sub-committee - Research Ethics Committee/Board/Panel (UK/Canada/Australia) - Medical Ethics Committee - Human Subjects/Research Committee / Human Research Ethics (Advisory) Panel - Committee for/on the Protection of Human Subjects - Committee on (Health) Research Ethics - Office of Research Ethics - Abbreviations: IEC, REC, REB, ERB, METC, DEC. REC/REB/ERB/DEC are short enough to collide with unrelated jargon (e.g. REC as a recognition-heuristic model abbreviation, DEC as a decision-task label, Reb as an author surname), so these four require an ethics/approval/committee/board/panel/institutional/ protocol word within ~30 characters in the same sentence. IRB and IEC are kept unconstrained as they are not observed to collide in practice. - Dutch: Medisch-ethische toetsingscommissie (METC) - French: Comité d'éthique / Comité d'Ethique

Animal research committee names - Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee / IACUC (US standard) - Any sentence containing animal(s) and committee (or board/panel) within ~5 words of each other — catches Experimental Animal Care and Use Committee, Animal Ethics Committee, Animal Welfare Board, Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body, etc. - UK: Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body / AWERB - Dutch: Dierexperimentencommissie / DEC (see DEC constraint above); Instantie voor Dierenwelzijn / IvD - German: Tierversuchskommission / TvK (advisory committee under §15 Tierschutzgesetz) - French: Comité d'éthique en expérimentation animale / CEEA; Comité d'éthique animale - Spanish: Comité de ética en experimentación animal / CEEA

Approval phrasing - Ethics/Ethical (approval|clearance) [was] (granted|obtained|received|given|secured) - Ethically approved - Approved by the (ethics|irb|institutional|independent|local|review|research) ... - This/The study/protocol/experiment/procedures/methods/research [was] (approved|reviewed) by - Study was ethically approved - (Followed|met|follow) the ethical (guidelines|standards|requirements) - In accordance with ... ethical (guidelines|standards) - Ethics protocol - Approved under (protocol|reference|number)

Waiver / exemption - Ethics/Ethical (waiver|exemption) - IRB (waiver|exemption|exempt) - Exempted from (ethics|irb|institutional review) - Waived by the (ethics|irb|institutional|review) (committee|board) - Deemed exempt

Declaration of Helsinki - Declaration of Helsinki / Helsinki Declaration

Patterns are NOT designed to capture: - General ethical considerations or ethical dilemmas in study content - Mentions of “ethics” in theoretical or philosophical contexts - Author approval of a manuscript (e.g. “all authors approved the final version”) - Ethical behaviour as a measured variable (e.g. “ethical decision-making”) - URLs containing “ethics” in path components

Live data collection is detected by the internal helper .detect_live_data(), which searches for sentences indicating that data were collected directly from human or animal participants. Papers without any live-data signal are not flagged as missing ethics approval. The helper uses conservative patterns to avoid flagging secondary data analyses, computational experiments, or economics papers that analyse existing survey data. See .detect_live_data() for the full list of patterns and intentional exclusions.

A paper that states that no human or animal subjects took part (“no human subjects were involved”, “did not involve human participants”, or people taking part “not as subjects”) is not expected to have an ethics statement, whatever participant phrase it also has (see .detect_no_participants()). A negation near a participant phrase alone (“No participants were excluded”) does not count: it reports a study with participants.

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Power Analysis Check works

This module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. If specified by the user, it also uses a large language model (LLM) to extract information reported in power analyses, including the statistical test, sample size, alpha level, desired level of power, and magnitude and type of effect size.

The Power Analysis Check module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. Without the use of an LLM, the module uses regular expressions to classify the power analysis as a-priori, sensitivity or post-hoc. With the use of an LLM, it checks if the power analysis is reported with all required information.

The regular expressions can miss power analyses, or fail to classify them correctly. The type of power analysis is often difficult to classify, which can easily be solved by explicitly specifying the type of power analysis as ‘a-priori’, ‘sensitivity’, or ‘post-hoc’. Note that ‘post-hoc’ or ‘observed’ power is rarely useful. The LLM can fail to identify information in the paper, and will not have access to information in paragraphs in the paper other than those that contain the word ‘power’. This package was validated by the Metacheck team on articles in Psychological Science.

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine, Daniel Lakens and Cristian Mesquida

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 128 papers with 246 instances of power statements, 203 were correctly detected (true positives), 22 were missed (false negatives) and 21 were incorrectly detected (false positives). Overall, among all instances flagged as power statements, 90.6% were correct (positive predictive value).

How Preregistration Check works

Retrieve information from preregistrations in a standardised way, and make them easier to check.

The Preregistration Check module identifies preregistrations on the OSF and AsPredicted based on links in the manuscript, retrieves the preregistration text, and organizes the information into a template. The module retrieves text from the OSF with its API, and from AsPredicted with ResearchBox’s API when a ResearchBox key is set (see rbox_token()), or else from the preregistration’s web page. The information in the preregistration is returned. An AsPredicted link that could not be read is named in the report, with the reason.

The module can’t extract information from non-structured preregistration templates (i.e., where the preregistration is uploaded in a single text field) and it can’t retrieve information in preregistrations that are stored as text documents on the OSF.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download information from other preregistration sites, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Marginal Significance works

List all sentences that describe an effect as ‘marginally significant’.

The marginal module searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The list of terms is a subset of those listed in a blog post by Matthew Hankins. The module returns all sentences that match terms describing ‘marginally significant’ results.

Some of the terms identified might not be problematic in some contexts, and there are ways to describe ‘marginal significance’ that are not detected by the module.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 51 papers with 87 statements, this module correctly identified 38 statements (true positives) and incorrectly flagged 22 statements (false positives). It failed to detect 27 statements. Thus, among all statements flagged by the module, 63% were genuine cases (positive predictive value). However, the module missed 42% of all true statements (false negative rate).

How StatCheck works

Check consistency of p-values and test statistics

The Statcheck module runs Statcheck. Statcheck searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern, and identifies APA reported statistical tests. More information on the package can be found at https://github.com/cran/statcheck. The module only returns Statcheck results for t-tests and F-tests, as these are the only tests which have been validated, see https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/.

Statcheck was developed by Michèle Nuijten and Sascha Epskamp.

Statcheck considers p = 0.000 an error, as you should report p < 0.001. Furthermore, p < 0.03 is an error if the p-value was 0.031, and one should simply report exact p-values (p = 0.031). Statcheck might miss one-sided tests, and falsely assume the p-value is incorrect. For more information, see StatCheck.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 685 tests with 34 instances of inconsistent reporting, Statcheck correctly detected 34 of them, and incorrectly identified 26. Therefore 0% of true instances were missed, and 43% of detections were false positives. See https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/ for more details of the validation.

How Exact P-Values works

List any p-values reported with insufficient precision (e.g., p < .05 or p = n.s.) or reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000).

This module uses regular expressions to identify p-values. It will flag any values reported as p > ? or p < numbers greater than .001. It will also flag p-values reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000, p = 0.00), which are mathematically impossible — p-values are never exactly zero and should instead be reported as p < .001.

A p-value that is not the result of a test is not flagged: a table note (“* p < .05, ** p < .01”), a figure legend (“Asterisks indicate significant differences (p < .05)”, “Values in bold are significant at p < .05”) or a stated threshold (“p < .05 was considered significant”, “thresholded at p < .001”). GROBID often puts such notes into the running text, so each p-value is judged by the words in its own clause (the text up to a “;” or a full stop), and never left out when a test statistic belongs to it (“F(1, 7) = 8.03, # p < .05”). The column not_a_test of the table says why a p-value was left out (“marker”, “threshold” or “legend”). Notes in other forms, such as a marker after the p-value (“p < .05 (*)“), are still flagged.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (“decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables (as opposed to a table’s footnote text, which the module does see and tries to exclude — see above).

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine and Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 225 papers containing 405 instances of non-exact p-values, the module correctly detected 269 cases (true positives) and incorrectly identified 78 (false positives). It missed 136 instances of imprecisely reported p-values (false negatives) and correctly identified 4557 cases of precisely reported p-values (true negative). Additionally, 78% of positive detections were correct (positive predictive value).

How Non-Significant P Value Check works

This module checks for imprecisely reported p values. If p > .05 is detected, it warns for misinterpretations.

The nonsignificant p-value check searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The module identifies all p-values in a manuscript and selects those that are not reported to be smaller than or equal to 0.05. It returns all sentences containing non-significant p-values.

In the future, the Metacheck team aims to incorporate a machine learning classifier to only return sentences likely to contain misinterpretations. If you want to help to improve the module, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 194 papers with 1602 instances of non-significant p-values, this module correctly detected 1486 of them, and incorrectly identified 153. Additionally, 91% of detections were true instances (positive predictive value). That is, when this module flags non-significant p-values in a paper, it correctly identifies an issue 91% of the time.

How Effect Sizes in t-tests and F-tests works

The Effect Size module checks if effect sizes are correctly reported in t-tests and F-tests.

The Effect Size check searches for regular expressions that match typical ways in which effect sizes are reported. It subsequently checks different ways in which Cohen’s d, g, ηp2, and ωp2 can be computed against the reported value. If effects are missing, or might be incorrect, you the module provides a warning. The module was validated on APA reported statistical tests, and might miss effect sizes that were reported in other reporting styles. It was validated by the Metacheck team on papers published in Psychological Science.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 161 papers with 1469 tests, this module correctly detected 1106 reported effect sizes (true positives) and correctly identified 295 cases where no effect size was present (true negatives). However, it missed 23 that were reported (false negatives), and incorrectly identified 45 effect sizes when none were reported (false positives). Among all instances detected by the module, 96% were true cases (positive predictive value). In a validation against 221 reported Cohen’s d effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 218 cases (99%). In a validation against 485 partial eta-squared effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 480 (99%)

How Codebook Check works

This module checks whether the data columns in a repository are documented in a codebook or README. It locates codebook/readme files, extracts the variable definitions they contain, matches those definitions against the data columns extracted by data_check, and reports documentation coverage: how many data columns are documented, and which documented variables are never used.

The Codebook Check module consumes the columns and file classification produced by data_check (and, transitively, repo_check). Files classified as codebook or readme are parsed with parse_codebook(), which reads structured tables (CSV/TSV/Excel with a variable-name column and a label column), embedded labels in haven files (SPSS/Stata/SAS), and plain text from rich-text formats (docx/pdf/rtf/odt). Embedded haven labels from data files are also harvested directly.

Each data column is then matched against the parsed variable definitions with match_column_labels(), using normalised-name matching with experiment-group scoping, haven-label priority, and rule-based label-equivalence merging.

Beyond the variable label, the module harvests the DDI-style per-variable properties a source supplies: the value labels / code list (the 1=“Strongly disagree”…5=“Strongly agree” mapping — from SPSS/Stata value labels and from a codebook “values”/“coding” column), the missing-value scheme (which codes denote missingness, from haven declared missings and labels that read as “refused”/“n/a”), and question text and columns when present. These are carried onto the matched data columns and exported into the Psych-DS variableMeasured (as a schema.org code list plus namespaced metacheck: fields).

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE). When llm_use(TRUE), three optional LLM tiers run: parsing unstructured codebook text that the rules could not handle, fuzzy-matching still-unlabelled columns to still-unmatched codebook variables, and merging conflicting label definitions into a single canonical label.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the codebook_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Repository Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories.

The Repository Check module lists files on the OSF, GitHub, ResearchBox, DSpace (PsychArchives and other legacy- and DSpace 7-based installations), Zenodo, Dataverse, Figshare, Dryad, ReShare, 4TU.ResearchData, Mendeley Data, DataONE (Arctic Data Center, KNB, and other member nodes), and LDbase based on links in the manuscript.

Listing a repository’s files never requires an API key, but some hosts answer faster or more completely with one: OSF allows 100 requests/hour unauthenticated versus 10,000/day with a token (see [osf_pat()]), and Dryad enforces a strict per-IP daily quota on repeated listing (see cache below). Dryad is a special case beyond rate limits: listing a Dryad dataset’s files works without a key, but actually downloading file CONTENTS from Dryad fails with HTTP 401 unless a key is set, even for a fully public dataset (see [dryad_pat()] or [dryad_auth()]). Since this module only lists files and never downloads their contents itself, that limitation applies to data_check and code_check (which do download content), not here – see their own documentation.

When a linked OSF page is a registration, its registered_from project (the one it was registered from, which the manuscript itself may never link directly) is also checked: if that project is public its files are listed under its own URL, and if it is closed or otherwise inaccessible the report flags it explicitly, since some registrations only describe their content rather than mirroring it.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download files from additional data repositories reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the repo_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Code Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories checked by repo_check about code files (R, Python, SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, MATLAB).

The Code Check module checks R (also R Markdown and Quarto), Python (also Jupyter notebooks), SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, and MATLAB files, using regular expressions to check the code; JASP files are listed but not read. The language of a Quarto document or a notebook is read from the file itself (its engine or kernel), not from its extension. The regular expression search will detect the number of comments, the lines at which libraries/imports are loaded, attempts to detect absolute paths to files, setwd() and install.packages() calls, and lists files that are loaded, and checks if these files are in the repository; R code is also parsed. The module will return suggestions to improve the code if there are no comments, if libraries/imports are loaded in lines further than 4 lines apart, if files that are loaded are not in the repository, if absolute file paths, setwd() or install.packages() are found, and if R code cannot be parsed.

The regular expressions can miss information in code files, or falsely detect parts of the code as a fixed file path. Libraries/imports might be loaded in one block, even if there are more than 3 intermittent lines. The module was tried out internally on papers published in Psychological Science, but has not been formally validated. There might be valid reasons why some loaded files can’t be shared, but the module can’t evaluate these reasons, and always gives a warning.

The module also checks whether the repository pins the R/package versions the analysis actually depended on: an renv.lock file (parsed for the R version and every locked package + its version/source), a sessionInfo()/sessioninfo::session_info() text dump (matched by filename — sessionInfo.txt/session_info.txt and similar, or embedded in a README), or a groundhog::groundhog.library()/checkpoint::checkpoint() date-pin call in the code (a bare library(groundhog)/library(checkpoint) does not count — the pinning call itself must be present). When none of these is found, the report flags it as a reproducibility gap: package versions may drift between when the analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt.

If you want to extend the package to perform additional checks on code files, or make the checks work on other types of code files, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

Checking code requires downloading its file contents. For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this never succeeds unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (listing the repository’s files does not need one). Without a key, every Dryad-hosted code file is listed but unchecked, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures recorded in the manifest.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Raphael Merz

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the code_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Data Check works

This module classifies the files in a repository into semantic types (data, codebook, code, software, output, supplemental, readme, asset, other) and, for tabular data files, extracts each column’s type and summary statistics.

The Data Check module consumes the file list produced by repo_check (or any files added via local_path). Each file is classified with rule-based heuristics layered over metacheck’s file_category() and file_types: file names matching README / codebook patterns win first, then an extension crosswalk, then format-locked extension overrides. For every file classified as tabular data that is available locally, the module reads the file head, describes each column with data_col_facets() and computes summary statistics for numeric columns. Every sheet of a spreadsheet that holds a table is read: a file with several such sheets has its columns under source_file = "file.xlsx [sheet]", and the sheet column names the sheet of any spreadsheet with several sheets; a sheet of notes or a codebook is treated as documentation when the file has another table. Following DDI, a column is described by orthogonal facets rather than a single type: representation (numeric/text/datetime/code — how it is stored), measurement_level (Stevens: nominal/ordinal/interval/ratio), concept (what it measures: reaction_time/age/gender/likert/id/…), role (identifier/measure/condition/timestamp), unit, and a quality state (ok/empty/constant). Concepts are detected by name+value rules (the demographic concepts via data_check_demographic()); when llm_use(TRUE) the model fills concepts and measurement levels the rules left blank. Qualtrics survey exports are recognised (data_check_is_qualtrics()): their extra header rows are stripped so columns type correctly, and the reserved response-metadata columns (StartDate, Duration, Finished, …) are tagged.

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE): columns the rules cannot resolve (ambiguous 3–20-unique integers, ambiguous character columns) fall back to continuous / text respectively. When llm_use(TRUE), ambiguous columns and unresolved file types (other) are sent to llm() for optional refinement, and every file is assigned a study group (ex1, pilot2, shared, …) so that multi-study repositories can be recognised (used by psychds_check). Without an LLM the study group is left NA (unknown).

Column extraction requires files to be readable from disk. Files fetched by repo_check from OSF/GitHub/etc. without a local copy (file_location is NA) are classified but not column-extracted; pass local_path to point at a downloaded copy of the repository.

For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this means column extraction never happens unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (dataset listing and metadata do not need one). Without a key, every Dryad file stays classified but un-downloaded, column_n is 0, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures. A one-time session message is also shown (see repo_check) the first time a Dryad dataset is encountered with no key set.

Every downloaded text file the authors wrote (code, settings, survey definitions such as Qualtrics .qsf files, documentation, outputs) is searched for keys: API keys, access tokens and private keys, which let anyone who downloads the file use a paid service at the authors’ cost. Of a tabular data file only the column names and first 3 rows of the table as read are searched: its values come from participants, but a key the authors set for a whole survey (as embedded data in a Qualtrics survey flow) is exported in every response. The search uses the rules of the secret scanner betterleaks (https://github.com/betterleaks/betterleaks, MIT licence) of high and medium confidence. A key found turns the traffic light red; the report and the keys table give its file, line and type, and only its beginning. Other files over 25 MB and binary files are not searched. A key is never tested with its provider. Every module’s result has any whole key replaced by “keyremoved” (see module_run()).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Levi Baruch

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the data_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Reproducibility Check works

This module assesses whether a paper’s R and Python code can be run on its data. It works in two phases. The static phase (always run) collects the package dependencies the code declares, rewrites the data-file paths that do not work in the repository as the authors organised it, works out the order the scripts must run in, and diagnoses why any referenced input file is unavailable — reporting what a reproduction attempt would involve and where it is likely to break. The execution phase (opt-in, execute = TRUE) then actually runs the code: it copies the repository into a throwaway directory, optionally installs the declared dependencies into a throwaway library, runs each script in order in an isolated subprocess with a per-script timeout, and captures the outcome and full output of each run.

By default (execute = FALSE) the module does not run any code — it performs only static analysis: reading dependencies with the same extractors as code_check, finding the files the code references in the repository, and building a run-order dependency graph. It reports what would be installed and run rather than doing it.

When execute = TRUE, the module additionally runs the downloaded code on your machine — a deliberate, opt-in action gated behind that argument (callr is required for R code). Each script runs in an isolated subprocess so a crash cannot take down the R session; note this isolates crashes, not the filesystem or network. Installs (when install_missing = TRUE) and the throwaway run library never touch your main R library. Each script’s outcome is one of ran_ok, errored, timed_out, dependency_unavailable, not_parsed, run_by_source (an R script another script runs with source(), which is run only inside that script, since on its own it lacks the packages and objects the sourcing script sets up first), run_by_import (a Python file other files import, a module rather than a script, unless it has an if __name__ == "__main__": block), jags_model (a JAGS or BUGS model saved with an R extension, which another script reads) or not_r_content (a file that is not R at all, such as a JSON or HTML dump); these last two are not run and are not failures. python2 marks Python 2 code that was not run. With sandbox = "docker" such code runs with Python 2.7 (option metacheck.docker_image_py2; FALSE turns this off), which modifications records; it is never converted, since that can change results. defines_functions is a file that only defines functions, which is used, not run (table$used_by names the scripts that use it), and not_analysis is a file of software rather than of the analysis: a package’s code, tests, documentation or setup.py, a copy of a tool, a web application, a script a program wrote (table$not_analysis_why says which); neither is run or counted as a script. docker_failed means the container never started, which says nothing about the script, so it keeps the light from being green but does not make it red. needs_a_person is a script that stopped because it asks a person to choose a file or answer a question, or that starts an app, and needs_internet one that stopped because the network is off in a Docker run, or, with internet = TRUE, could not reach what it asked for; download_too_large is a script stopped for downloading more than the limits allow (see internet), and out_of_memory one that, or a program it started (such as a compiler), ran out of the memory each script may use. All four are treated the same way. not_downloaded is a script that stopped on a file the repository lists but that was not downloaded, such as one over max_file_size: it could not be tried, so it is not counted as a script that did not reproduce (summary_table$repro_not_downloaded_n counts them apart). not_read is a file metacheck could not read, so it was not run; the light is then not green, as the file may read and run on another computer, and table$error_message says why, naming a package that is installed but cannot be loaded on the computer that ran the check. empty is a file with nothing in it, which is not run or counted as a script. For a script that did not run through, table$error_message is its error, short (at most 300 characters): the error line of its output, which names a package that is not there, with the address it asked for when it needed the internet, or the time limit it reached; table$reason sorts it into a kind. A timeout means “still running at the cutoff”, not a failure, so raise timeout for legitimately long-running (e.g. Bayesian) scripts. dependency_unavailable means the script’s own package could not be installed from live CRAN, the CRAN Archive, or a named GitHub/URL source — an infrastructure limitation, not a defect in the paper’s code, so unlike errored/timed_out it does not force the traffic light red. The full stdout/stderr of every run is kept in the result’s run_results (the report shows it in per-script dropdowns, capped for readability). The traffic light reflects the STATIC readiness signals by default, but an execution error (errored/timed_out) does recolour it to red — a script that crashes on its own analysis is a genuine reproduction failure. A script that fails with could not find function is run once more with the library() calls the project’s other scripts make added at its top (or the one package that exports the function, or magrittr for %>%), and a script that then runs is reported as reproducible only after that repair.

Dependencies are reported name-only, with the source (CRAN, GitHub, or a base package already shipped with R) — static analysis of the code cannot know which version was used, and running against current package versions is itself informative: a break argues the authors should have pinned versions. When the repository does record versions, a run with recorded_versions = TRUE installs the packages the scripts load at those versions, but it does not change the R version, which remains a limitation of the check.

Run order is derived from read-after-write data dependencies (a script writing a file another reads must run first), source() edges, and numeric filename prefixes as a weak tie-breaker. Ambiguous orderings, dependency cycles, and inputs a script reads but that no earlier script produces (and that are not in the repository) are surfaced as warnings, since these are the situations most likely to make an otherwise-correct reproduction fail.

Python code (.py files, and notebooks and Quarto documents whose code is Python, from which the code is extracted first) goes through the same two phases, reported in its own “Python code” section. Each file is checked for syntax errors with Python’s own parser when a Python interpreter (python, or else python3) is available; dependencies are read from the import statements, with versions from a requirements.txt or pyproject.toml when there is one, and installed with pip; each script runs as its own python process (processx is required), so unlike R scripts, a name one script defines is not available to another. When no Python interpreter is available, the Python files are reported as not run and the R results are still returned. Statistics the Python scripts print are compared with the paper together with the R output and any self-reproducible output (JASP/jamovi, SPSS-Viewer, notebook outputs).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How PubPeer Comments works

This module checks references and warns for citations that have comments on pubpeer (excluding Statcheck comments).

The PubPeer module uses the PubPeer API to check for each reference that has a DOI whether there are comments on the post-publication peer review platform. If comments are found, a link to the comments is provided. Comments by ‘Statcheck’ on PubPeer are ignored, see https://retractionwatch.com/2016/09/02/heres-why-more-than-50000-psychology-studies-are-about-to-have-pubpeer-entries/.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

For more information, see PubPeer.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How RetractionWatch works

This module checks references and warns for citations in the RetractionWatch Database.

The RetractionWatch Check module compares the reference list against studies in the RetractionWatch database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that the study was retracted, has an expression of concern, or a correction.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the authors are already aware that a study was retracted, but the module can’t evaluate this.

The database can be manually updated with the rw_update function. For more information, see https://gitlab.com/crossref/retraction-watch-data.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Replication Check works

This module checks references and warns for citations of original studies for which replication or reproduction studies exist in the FLoRA database.

The Replication Check module compares the reference list against studies in the FLoRA (FORRT Library of Replication Attempts) database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that a replication or reproduction of the original study exists, and should be cited. No reminder is given when the paper already cites that replication or reproduction (matched by its DOI).

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the original study was cited for other reasons than the empirical claim tested, or that the replication/reproduction in the FLoRA database is for only one of the studies in the paper, and not the study the authors discuss.

The database can be manually updated with the FLoRA_update() function. For more information, see https://forrt.org/FLoRA/.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens, Lisa DeBruine and Lukas Wallrich

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Full text

Abstract

Background. Whether multi-agent large language model (LLM) pipelines help, add nothing, or underperform a single call may depend on the input and scoring contract rather than the architecture; these boundary conditions are unmapped.

We compared a single direct call (A×1), five personas in one context (P), and five isolated roles integrated by a moderator (MDT) across curated corpora (87 clinicopathological cases, 406 MedCaseReasoning cases, 392 replication vignettes) and two disjoint emergency cohorts from one repository (364 mixed-label encounters; a protocol-frozen 400-encounter disease-label-only replacement, one local serving stack). Scoring was judge-mediated top-k working-label matching. A completed factorial isolated the active ingredient; a symptompermissive output-contract arm tested contract fit; three physicians rated discordant cases.

Findings. On curated corpora MDT improved differential-list recall (top-3: +5•5 points, p = 0•0079; +3•0, p = 0•0079; +3•3, p = 0•0013) but not top-1 accuracy; the factorial attributed the gain to independent generation plus moderated synthesis, not personas. On emergency presentations the sign reversed: in the disease-label-only cohort A×1 led MDT at every endpoint (top-3 -5•2 points, 95% CI -8•0 to -2•4, p = 0•0008), a second family reproduced it, and bridged cases excluded the serving stack. A symptom-permissive contract shrank the deficit's point estimates by 52-75%, replacement-cohort intervals all spanning zero though remaining negative; the clause harmed no arm. Physician safety ratings were inconclusive (original-cohort gate missed; replacement-cohort rescue contrast unadjudicable, 95% CI -32•7 to +2•6).

Interpretation. Multi-agent diagnostic gains are real but conditional: the sign of the effect tracks input genre, and its magnitude tracks output-contract fit. Curated-benchmark gains should not be Implications of all the available evidence. Gains measured on curated benchmarks should not be extrapolated to first-contact clinical text. Where multi-call designs are used on uncurated input, plain independent samples with a moderating call avoid the role-list penalty. An output contract permitting undifferentiated symptom-level entries is free-it harmed no arm-and is a defensible default wherever reference labels may be working diagnoses. Strategy selection by input, model, and endpoint remains a hypothesis requiring prospective evaluation, with independent emergency datasets and adequately powered safety studies.

[div-01]

extrapolated to first-contact emergency text, where role-based teams underperform a single call at seven-fold token cost. The reversal is label-level and judge-mediated; clinical safety is neither established nor excluded.

Funding. None.

Funding. None.

Research in context

Evidence before this study. Multi-agent LLM diagnostic pipelines report gains, but specialist personas, independent generation, synthesis, and additional test-time compute are usually varied together, so the active ingredient is unidentified. Evaluation is concentrated on curated case reports, leaving performance on first-contact notes and working-diagnosis labels unclear, and no study has tested whether the output contract demanded of the model contributes to measured underperformance.

Added value of this study.

Across curated corpora and two disjoint emergency cohorts, we show the multi-agent effect is conditional and map two of its moderators. A completed factorial attributes the curated gain to independent generation plus moderated synthesis, not personas, and confines it to differential-list recall. The emergency deficit is established on a protocol-frozen cohort whose labels are all disease-level diagnoses, reproduced by a second model family on the same cases, and insensitive to the serving stack on bridged cases-so neither symptom-level labels nor the stack explains it. A symptom-permissive output contract closes the breadth component of the deficit while the ordering component persists. Physician safety ratings are inconclusive by design.

Introduction

Diagnostic error is a leading source of preventable harm [1, 2], and large language models (LLMs) now perform competitively on benchmarks built for expert diagnosticians [3, 4, 5, 6, 7]. A frontier model has reached attending-level differential agreement on MGH Case Records [25], and direct assessments on standardized cases find differential construction the weakest reasoning stage [26].

A popular proposal simulates a team through specialist personas, conversational frameworks, or voting over independent generations [8, 9, 10, 11], drawing on multidisciplinary practice [12]. The evidence is mixed: conversational multi-agent frameworks report gains [8, 13], yet systemprompt personas show no stable improvement [14], debate can entrench error [15], and automatically assembled systems often underperform strong self-consistency baselines despite higher cost [17]. Single-call chain-of-thought prompting can also improve reasoning [16], while collaboration's benefit shrinks with capability [22] and tool-using clinical agents show modest or inconsistent gains at substantially higher cost [24].

Two issues remain entangled. "Multiple perspectives" can mean personas voiced sequentially in one context or independent opinions combined afterwards, but these configurations also differ in calls, synthesis, and intermediate output structure. In addition, evaluation concentrates on curated, information-rich case reports rather than emergency presentations, where input is incomplete and the reference is the treating clinician's working label. We compared single-call, sequentialpersona, and moderated-team strategies across corpora differing in difficulty, provenance, and label structure. Our primary contrasts were MDT-A×1 at top-3 and top-5. We treated factorial and persona contrasts as secondary, emergency performance as judge-mediated working-label matching, and cross-corpus patterns as descriptive.

Methods

Study design and datasets

We compared three prompting strategies on datasets differing in difficulty, provenance, and label structure; all received the same case text and returned a ranked top-5 differential, scored against the reference diagnosis. The development corpus was 87 CPC cases (2013-2016) [5], split into 41 prompt-design and 46 held-out. Additional curated evaluation used 406 MedCaseReasoning cases [20], a 2025 benchmark with acknowledged contamination risk. The third dataset, ER-Reason [21], contains real emergency encounters from a PhysioNet credentialed repository used under its data use agreement: from 3,984 encounters, after deduplication, we drew a chiefcomplaint-stratified random sample of 364 (fixed seed). The input is the provider note truncated before "Medical Decision Making"; labels exist at symptom (n = 168) and disease (n = 196) levels. In revision, a replacement primary cohort of 400 disease-label encounters was drawn from the same repository under a protocol frozen before any generation: all original-cohort cases excluded, every candidate label audited, fixed-seed chief-complaint-stratified sampling, identical prompts and temperatures, one local serving stack, five seeds (self-frozen protocol; Statistical analysis); the original-cohort analysis is retained unchanged (Supplementary Note S39).

Encounter text was processed on self-hosted infrastructure; adjudication exchanged only labels and candidate strings.

Workup-informed input condition

To test whether additional information changed the emergency result, we appended each encounter's objective results to its presentation (prompts, model, decoding, seeds, judge unchanged); filters, leakage checks, and the label-in-text analysis are in Supplementary Methods S-M8.

Condensed-report, filler-arm, and replication-cohort experiments

To compare input forms on fixed content, we harvested full narrative case reports from PubMed Central and rewrote them into teaching vignettes under a condenser prompt forbidding disclosure of the reference diagnosis, with a zero-tolerance dual audit, an exploratory length-matched filler arm, and a protocol-frozen expansion to 392 replication vignettes; construction details and the selection-asymmetry disclosure are in the supplement (Supplementary Methods S-M6; Supplementary Note S35).

Prompting strategies and control arms

The primary CPC, MedCaseReasoning, and emergency comparisons used qwen3.8-flash (176B, provider-documented, thinking disabled) and returned a ranked top-5 list. A×1 is a single zeroshot call at requested temperature 0; P reasons sequentially from five perspectives (attending physician, pathophysiology, imaging and laboratory, epidemiology and exposure, skeptic) in one shared context at 0•3; MDT issues the same five roles as independent, mutually blinded calls integrated by a moderator (six calls). P and MDT share roles, model, and prompt material but differ in call count, candidate independence, moderator synthesis, and intermediate output structure, so P-MDT is not a single-variable contrast. Control arms included A×1+CoT, fivesample self-consistency with Borda (A×5), A×5+Mod, MDT-Borda, P-split, moderator-anatomy ablations, a prompt-corrected Pfixed, a larger backbone, and the deepseek-v4.1-flash and gpt-5.1 families (Supplementary Tables S10-S12,

Models, decoding, and compliance routing

Every ER-Reason arm was regenerated on self-hosted 4-bit infrastructure with prompts byteidentical to the curated runs and no case content leaving the machine; the single cloud pathway (the same-cohort deepseek replication) used audited zero-data-retention routing pinned to one provider. Because cloud endpoints need not honour requested temperature 0, the case, not the run, is the unit of inference (self-hosted runs are exact greedy). Deployment details, the temperaturematched reanalysis, and the output-contract sensitivity arm are in the supplement (Supplementary Methods S-M7; Supplementary Notes S36 and S39).

LLM judge and validation

Scoring used one frozen LLM judge per candidate-reference pair [19], one cached verdict per pair; the primary judge (GLM-5.3-flash) and rules were frozen before outcome analyses (shared cache 253,005 pairs). Clinician-annotation samples assessed judge-clinician agreement, initially on a disjoint 100-pair batch rated by two blinded senior physicians and then on other corpora and the self-hosted rerun (Supplementary Tables S5, S5-2, S5-3). A second judge (deepseek-v4.1-flash) applied identical rules as a sensitivity analysis (disagreement 5•69% overall, 3•6-5•7% by stratum). Pairing does not eliminate arm-dependent misclassification (Supplementary Table S8).

Blinded physician rating of the discordant pairs

Three non-author senior physicians (≥1 emergency medicine) independently rated all 34 emergency cases where the strategies' top-1 verdicts disagreed plus 30 concordant controls under a frozen protocol with a pre-unblinding agreement gate (design and blinding: Supplementary Methods S-M9; Supplementary Note S37).

Blinded physician rating on the replacement cohort

Because the primary emergency analysis moved to the replacement cohort, the safety assessment was repeated there under a protocol (v1.3) frozen before any rating material existed, with the primary question inverted to case-level rescue of the losing strategy's list and the danger comparison retained as secondary (sampling frame and instruments: Supplementary Methods S-M10; Supplementary Note S39).

Statistical analysis

The pre-specified primary endpoints were top-3 and top-5 recall; endpoints, primary judge, and comparison families were fixed before the comparisons were run; later control arms are labelled sensitivity analyses. The study was not publicly registered; pre-specification rests on dated, append-only cache milestones and self-frozen protocol versions. Tests use per-case hit rates across seeds (paired two-sided Wilcoxon; zero differences dropped) with 95% case-level cluster bootstrap intervals (10,000 resamples). Benjamini-Hochberg correction applies within two prespecified families: the original 27 framework contrasts (12 of 14 raw p <0•05 survive) and the replacement cohort's three protocol-frozen primary contrasts (adjusted p 0•0159, 0•0024, 0•0129).

Battery arms report raw p, as pre-committed.

Results

Judge agreement with clinicians is corpus-dependent but stable across emergency S5, S5-2, S5-3; Note S39).

MDT improves differential-list recall, not top-1 accuracy, on curated corpora

On 87 CPC cases, MDT exceeded A×1 at both primary endpoints (top-3 +5•5 points, 95% CI 1•1 to 10•3, p = 0•0079; top-5 +4•4, p = 0•015) (Table 1, Figures 1 and 2); no held-out 46-case contrast was significant (Supplementary Figure S2a). On 406 MedCaseReasoning cases MDT again improved top-3 (+3•0, 0•9 to 5•1, p = 0•0079) and top-5 (+3•9, p = 3•8 × 10) without a top-1 difference (p = 0•25); temperature-matched analyses preserved the advantage (Table 1). S6; Figure 2c; Note S39).

A second model family reproduced the reversal on both cohorts: deepseek-v4.1-flash on the same 364 cases (top-3 -7•5, p = 1•8 × 10; same-cohort replication, not external clinical validation; Supplementary Table S11) and, on the fresh cohort under one local stack, the same ordering independently (-6•2/-7•1/-6•5 at top-1/3/5, p ≤ 0•0003); a fixed-50-case family bridge shifted the MDT-A×1 gap by 0•0 points at every endpoint between cloud and local servings (Note S39).

Adding objective results did not close the gap: in the original cohort they raised every strategy (+2•8 to +8•0 at top-1/3/5) but the top-3 strategy-by-input interaction disfavoured MDT (p = 0•027; Supplementary Table S6, Figure 2c), persisting after exclusion of 20 label-in-text cases These estimates are consistent with heterogeneity across input formats but do not identify curation as its driver, exclude length effects, or support a dose-response curve: model, deployment, labels, provenance, and contamination all differ across corpora (Figure 4; Supplementary Note S35).

Output-contract fit tracks the magnitude: a safe one-sentence partial fix Because the strict contract demands disease-level entries while many emergency labels are working diagnoses, we tested a symptom-permissive contract-one appended sentence permitting undifferentiated entries over unsupported disease guesses (Methods; plan frozen before generation). On the original cohort it closed the breadth end of the symptom-stratum deficit (top-5 -9•6 points, p = 0•0036, to -3•2, interval spanning zero) while the ordering end persisted. On the fresh cohort, where every label is a disease diagnosis, the same contract narrowed the gap at every endpoint (point estimates shrinking 52-75%, all intervals spanning zero; gap shift +3•0 at top-5, p = 0•06): part of the reversal is an artefact of forced disease guessing under the strict contract on which the pre-specified primary result rests. The clause was safe and selective: on the original cohort it added +6•4 top-5 points to MDT and +5•1 to P (both p S3; Supplementary Tables S11, S17). These model-dependent P results argue against a general persona penalty. Non-thinking MDT consumed 6•8-8•4 times A×1's tokens (Table 2).

Interpretation

Whether a multi-agent team helped, added nothing, or underperformed depended on configuration, not architecture. On curated corpora the moderated team improved the differential's breadth without robustly improving its head; the factorial attributed the gain to independent generation plus moderated synthesis. On uncurated emergency presentations the sign reversed-persisting with all disease-level labels, in a second model family, and across serving stacks-and multi-call inference is not the cause: five plain samples under the same moderator matched the single call while the role-based team sat 6•8 points below it at top-3. Two moderators organise this heterogeneity. Input genre: the margin ran from positive on curated vignettes through null on self-disclosing narratives to negative on uncurated emergency text, the within-case contrast pointing the same way. Output-contract fit is the actionable moderator: a one-sentence clause permitting undifferentiated entries closed the breadth component of the emergency deficit (halving point estimates even where every label was a disease diagnosis), the ordering component persisting. Two strictnesses must be distinguished: scoring strictness fell on both arms and explains the gap in neither direction; contract strictness was not arm-neutral-the clause preferentially restored the team's recall (+4 to +5 versus +1 to +2 points), the signature of a dispersed candidate list forced to guess beyond the evidence. The reversal is therefore partly an artefact of forced differentiation and partly a genuine ordering deficit; the frozen-protocol primary result rests on the strict contract shared with the other corpora. The safety evidence, inconclusive on both cohorts, establishes neither clinical equivalence nor that the reversal is merely artefactual.

These results reconcile mixed literature: positive studies generated contributions separately and aggregated them [8, 13]; negative studies voiced personas in one context [14] or debated without feedback [15]; reports of models beating physicians on emergency cases used complete records and a comprehensiveness score that rewarded inclusion without penalising irrelevance [27], unlike our first-contact working-label design. Collaboration benefits vary with capability and reasoning configuration [22, 23]; agent count and persona framing show no uniform effect across clinical tasks [17, 18, 24].

Curated-benchmark gains should not be extrapolated to first-contact clinical input-the one setting whose labels no model could have memorised. On uncurated input, plain independent samples with a moderating call are the defensible multi-call form, and a contract permitting undifferentiated entries is a defensible default wherever labels may be working diagnoses.

Strategy selection by input, model, endpoint, and reasoning mode remains a routing hypothesis for prospective evaluation; and because the reversal is label-level, judge-mediated, and clinically unadjudicated, these results argue for caution about deployment claims, not clinical harm.

Several limitations bound inference. The primary emergency input stops before objective results, and the workup condition added objective content only; findings do not extend to complete-chart reasoning. Both emergency cohorts come from one repository, use working diagnoses, and were scored by an imperfect judge-the replacement cohort removes label granularity but shares repository, working-label construct, and judge, whose clinician-validated κ on the new candidate distribution (0•54-0•56 against clinician-clinician 0•85) matches the original, leaving the reversal resting partly on a noisy instrument (Note S39). The second-family result is same-cases model replication, not external validation; the same-rule second judge is a sensitivity analysis, not independent clinical validation; and the annotation samples support no unbiased arm-specific false-accept/false-reject estimates. Both physician ratings were inconclusive: the original-cohort danger, omission, and preference families missed the agreement gate, and the replacement-cohort rescue contrast was unadjudicable (95% CI -32•7 to +2•6 points; power limited to ~35-40-point differences; D5 descriptive; 8 of 83 Part A units with a pre-declared seed-1 display deviation). In the original cohort the deficit was directionally present in both label strata but separable only in the symptom stratum (top-3 and top-5 q = 0•049 and 0•022), and its magnitude was servingstack-sensitive (top-3 -9•0 cloud to -6•4 self-hosted, 4-bit quantisation a candidate explanation), though bridged cases bounded the stack contribution (50 cases, one seed; Note S39). The outputcontract arm was added after the primary analyses under a pre-generation frozen plan, and its fresh-cohort intervals all span zero. Clinician judge assessment did not cover the closed-model arms; part of the condensed-corpus adjudication used an alternative endpoint (94•99% agreement after equivalence checking). Curated-corpus contamination and strategy-by-memorisation interactions cannot be excluded. Cross-corpus contrasts confound model, deployment, labels, provenance, and contamination. Analyses were not externally registered, and thinking arms were post hoc, excluded the emergency corpus, and yielded corpus-specific results.

The full analysis pipeline (prompts, per-arm scripts, run files, and both judge caches) is openly released (GitHub; Zenodo DOI at publication). Ethics

This study is a secondary analysis of existing, de-identified data with no human-subjects recruitment and no access to identifiable information, and the institutional ethics committee reviewed the protocol and granted an exemption from institutional review board review (No. I-26ZM0145). It was conducted in accordance with the ethical standards of the 1964 Declaration of Helsinki and its later amendments. ER-Reason consists of clinical notes de-identified by the UCSF Information Commons under HIPAA Safe Harbor and Expert Determination methods and was used under the PhysioNet data use agreement. Consistent with PhysioNet's guidance on credentialed data and large language models, all automated processing of these de-identified records met the guidance's conditions for acceptable automated processing: the emergencycorpus arms ran on self-hosted infrastructure with no transmission of case content off the machine, and the one replication that sent case text to a cloud provider used a zero-data-retention endpoint (no retention, no training use, no human review of case content), with the serving provider logged and audited on every request. An amendment documenting this processing pathway was approved by the data provider's credentialing committee before the replication ran.

The physician rating component involved three practising clinicians assessing anonymised diagnosis lists derived from the same de-identified corpora on a local server, with no patient involvement and no case content leaving the rating environment; it is covered by the same exemption (No. I-26ZM0145).

cohorts

Before strategy comparison, the judge was assessed against two blinded clinicians on 100 development-untouched pairs (judge-clinician κ 0•814/0•878; inter-rater 0•812), with extension to other corpora (emergency symptom-level labels: inter-rater 0•612, judge-clinician 0•640/0•580; boundary-enriched rerun: 0•877, 0•580/0•540). The same design on the fresh cohort reproduced these values (0•560/0•540; 0•849), leaving measured judge reliability unchanged for the replacement analysis. Residual discordance was directional: clinicians endorsed 20 of 50 pairs the judge rejected but rejected only 3 of 50 it accepted, mostly concurrent or aetiologic candidates encompassing the reference; a full-corpus check found reference-containing false rejects running against the single call (8•8% vs 3•4%, p = 0•012), biasing the gap toward the null (Supplementary Tables

The original P arm scored below A×1 on CPC at every endpoint without significance; against Pfixed, MDT's CPC advantages were likewise non-significant (top-3 6•0, p = 0•0599; top-5 4•6, p = 0•0676), on MedCaseReasoning borderline at top-3 (+2•4, p = 0•0501) and significant at top-5 (+3•6, p = 0•0007). Independent generation plus moderated synthesis, not personas, carries the curated gain In the completed factorial (Figure 3a), mechanical aggregation never helped: Borda over five single-call samples scored 9•5 points below A×1 on MedCaseReasoning top-1, and reaggregating team lists did not help either. The same moderator over plain independent samples (A×5+Mod) recovered the advantage and matched the role-based team; A×1+CoT changed nothing (all p ≥0•61) and A×5 did not reproduce MDT (all p ≤0•059). With the moderatoranatomy controls, the pattern is consistent with independent generation plus moderated synthesis. On emergency text, plain samples plus a moderator exceeded MDT by 6•8 points at top-3 and 7•0 at top-5 without differing from A×1 (appendix). On emergency presentations the effect reverses-and survives three stress tests On the protocol-frozen 400-case emergency cohort whose reference labels are all disease-level diagnoses (Methods), A×1 outperformed MDT at every endpoint: top-1 51•7% versus 47•2% (-4•5 points, 95% CI -7•8 to -1•3, p = 0•016), top-3 -5•2 (-8•0 to -2•4, p = 0•0008), top-5 -4•0 (-6•7 to -1•5, p = 0•0086); majority-vote McNemar concordant, all three surviving in-family correction (adjusted p 0•0159, 0•0024, 0•0129). P also trailed A×1 on recall (top-3 -3•8, p = 0•047; top-5 -3•9, p = 0•021) and MDT added nothing over P, reproducing A×1 > P ≥ MDT at 7•3 times the single call's token cost. On 50 bridged original-cohort cases the MDT-A×1 estimate shifted within noise (top-3 +2•0, top-5 +6•0, intervals spanning zero; both stacks negative), so the reversal is not a stack artefact and does not rest on symptom-level labels (Supplementary Note S39). The original-cohort analysis (364 encounters, two stacks, 46% symptom-level labels) is retained in full: A×1 beat MDT at every endpoint (top-1 -3•9, p = 0•021; top-3 -6•4, p = 0•0022; top-5 -6•4, p = 0•0039) and in temperature-matched and judge-independent word-coverage checks (37•3% versus 33•0% P, 30•8% MDT; Supplementary Figure S2b)-lexical coverage being no clinical correctness measure (Supplementary Table 360 points, 95% CI -8•256 to -0•464, p = 0•0322).

On the fresh cohort the workup gain replicated for every strategy (+5•9 to +7•8, p ≤ 0•0002) and the reversal persisted (top-3 -4•9, p = 0•0031), but the interaction was null at every endpoint: the original cohort's negative interaction did not replicate (Note S39). Input genre tracks the sign of the effect (a descriptive map) Across corpora, MDT-A×1 top-3 point estimates ran +5•5 (CPC), +3•0 (MedCaseReasoning), +2•831 (condensed PMC vignettes, p = 0•0282), +1•108 (full PMC reports, p = 0•2134), and -6•4 (emergency notes) (Figure 4a). On the same 325 reports the paired condensed-minus-full interaction was +1•723 points (p = 0•2393) and the exploratory single-seed filler arm +3•1 (p = 0•096). The pooled 392-vignette replication cohort gave top-3 +3•3 (95% CI 1•5 to 5•1, p = 0•0013).

Model family, reasoning mode, and cost On CPC with deepseek-v4.1-flash, MDT had a non-significant top-1 advantage over A×1 (+4•8, p = 0•067) and exceeded P at top-3 and top-5 (p = 0•017, 0•023); on gpt-5.1, P exceeded A×1 at top-1 (+7•1, p = 0•0009) without a top-5 difference, while MDT exceeded A×1 at top-3 (+10•9, p = 0•0003); on the pooled replication cohort P again led at top-1 (+4•5, p <0•0001) and MDT improved top-3 (+3•3, p = 0•0013) (Supplementary Figure thinking-enabled single call closed the CPC non-thinking-team advantage and beat the nonthinking team on MedCaseReasoning, and under fully symmetric thinking the CPC team increments closed while the MedCaseReasoning top-3/5 advantages persisted; the full six-call thinking team used 73,866 tokens per case (4•92× the thinking single call).

These mode-matched, not token-budget-matched, contrasts are in Supplementary Note S38. Physician safety ratings are inconclusive Three physicians rated seed-1 lists for all 34 top-1-discordant original-cohort cases. S1 danger (mean AC1 0•185), dangerous-omission (0•423/0•458), and D5 preference (0•196) missed the 0•6 agreement gate; only misleading-inclusion ratings met it (0•834/0•726). The safety comparison is therefore inconclusive. Descriptively, 23 of 34 majority verdicts were ties (non-tied 6:5; MDTmore-dangerous rate 0•545, 95% CI 0•234 to 0•833); omission flags favoured A×1 (26•5% versus 14•7%), misleading-inclusion flags MDT (0% versus 14•7%), but power covered only preferences beyond ~72% (Supplementary Figure S2d; Supplementary Note S37). On the replacement cohort the redesigned rating passed the gate for every family except D5 preference (mean AC1 0•836 danger, 0•819/0•992 omission/inclusion flags, 0•822 rescue; D5 0•467, descriptive), with perfect intra-rater agreement (14/14 embedded repeats each). The primary question-did the losing list contain an acceptable entry encompassing the reference? was answered yes for 29 of 35 team-lost cases (82•9%) and 18 of 18 single-call-lost cases (100%); the -17•1-point difference (95% Newcombe CI -32•7 to +2•6) leaves the forceddifferentiation mechanism neither validated nor refuted, in the direction opposite to the hypothesis (baseline rescue on concordant misses 6/15, 40%). The secondary danger comparison was null (MDT more dangerous in 3 of 11 non-tied units, p = 0•23; omission flags 8:3, p = 0•23; misleading inclusion 0:0; no side preference in controls) (Supplementary Note S39).

Per-case ER-Reason outputs and the ER-Reason-derived entries of the judge caches are excluded from the public archive under ER-Reason's data use agreement, with case identifiers one-way hashed; the zero-retention routing audit log of the deepseek replication (provider per request) is retained by the authors and available on reasonable request.
